###### Part I. Pattern Analysis

1. Direct learners to examine each pattern carefully before classifying it.
2. Remind learners to use the defining characteristics of fractals, Fibonacci sequences, and the Golden Ratio when making classifications.
3. Encourage learners to justify every answer using observable features rather than guessing.
4. Circulate around the classroom and ask probing questions such as:
5. What feature helped identify the pattern?
6. Does the pattern repeat at different scales?
7. Is there evidence of a numerical sequence or proportional relationship?
8. Facilitate a brief discussion after completion and invite learners to explain their reasoning.
9. Clarify misconceptions regarding the differences among fractals, Fibonacci patterns, and Golden Ratio designs.

###### Part II. Manual Fractal Construction

1. Organize learners into pairs.
2. Verify that all required materials are available before the activity begins.
3. Explain the concept of iterations and demonstrate how a simple fractal evolves from Iteration 0 to subsequent iterations.
4. Encourage learners to plan the fractal design before drawing.
5. Monitor the construction process and ensure that each iteration follows a consistent rule.
6. Ask guiding questions such as:
  a. What rule is being repeated in every iteration?
  b. How does each iteration relate to the previous one?
  c. Which feature demonstrates self-similarity?
7. Encourage neatness, accuracy, and creativity in presenting the fractal.
8. Review the written descriptions and ensure that learners explain the mathematical features of their designs.

###### Part III. Digital Fractal Creation

1. Ensure that learners have access to the required devices and applications.
2. Demonstrate basic navigation of the selected software or online fractal generator.
3. Explain the expectations for the infographic output and the required descriptions.
4. Encourage experimentation with different parameters and design choices.
5. Monitor learner progress and provide technical assistance when needed.
6. Ask learners to compare the advantages of digital construction with manual construction.
7. Remind learners to save their outputs in the required format.
8. During presentations, encourage learners to explain: a. The rule used to generate the fractal.

-----

b. The appearance of self-similarity and recursion. c. Connections to nature, art, or design

###### C.1. Finding Practical Application

###### C. Demonstrating Knowledge and Activity C.1. Skills MAKING REAL-WORLD CONNECTIONS: CREATING FRACTAL PATTERN Instructions:

1. Work individually or in pairs to design and create an artwork inspired by any of the following mathematical concepts:
    a. Geometric symmetries
    b. Tessellations
    c. Frieze patterns
    d. Fibonacci sequence
    e. Golden Ratio
    f. Fractals
2. Choose your prefer medium (drawing, painting, digital design, collage, sculpture, etc.) and plan a layout that clearly shows the chosen mathematical concept.
3. Write short description (2-3 paragraphs) explaining the mathematics behind their artwork, including:
    a. The concept(s) applied
    b. How the concept is represented in the artwork
    c. Connections to nature, architecture, or design

#### 4. Submit your artwork and written explanation for display in a

class exhibit through gallery walk.

###### Processing Questions:

1. Which mathematical concept did you choose for your artwork, and why?
2. How does your artwork demonstrate the chosen concept (symmetry, tessellation, fractal, Fibonacci sequence, or Golden Ratio)?
3. How is your concept inspired by nature, art, or design?
4. What challenges did you encounter in applying mathematics creatively?
5. How does observing your classmates' artworks help you understand the different ways mathematics can be applied in creative contexts?

**Activity C.1. MAKING REAL-WORLD CONNECTIONS: CREATING FRACTAL PATTERN**

Before the Activity

1. Prepare art materials and ensure the availability of digital tools, if applicable.
2. Display sample artworks that demonstrate mathematical concepts.
3. Review the characteristics of geometric symmetries, tessellations, frieze patterns, Fibonacci sequence, Golden Ratio, and fractals.
4. Explain the expectations for the artwork, written explanation, and gallery walk.
5. Present the assessment criteria, emphasizing mathematical accuracy, creativity, craftsmanship, and quality of explanation.

###### During the Activity

Planning Stage

1. Allow learners to work individually or in pairs.
2. Encourage learners to select a mathematical concept that interests them.
3. Guide learners in brainstorming possible themes inspired by nature, architecture, technology, culture, or everyday objects.
4. Ask guiding questions such as:
    - Which mathematical concept will be featured in the artwork?
    - How will the concept be represented visually?
    - What real-world examples inspired the design?
5. Review initial plans before learners begin creating their artwork.

Creation Stage

1. Monitor learners as they construct their artwork.

-----

2. Encourage accurate application of the selected mathematical concept.
3. Provide assistance in using tools and materials when necessary.
4. Ask learners to explain the mathematical ideas being incorporated into their designs.
5. Encourage creativity while maintaining mathematical

integrity.

Writing Stage

1. Remind learners that the written explanation should clearly connect mathematics and art.
2. Encourage inclusion of:
3. The mathematical concept applied
4. How the concept appears in the artwork
5. Real-world connections to nature, architecture, technology, or design
6. Review drafts and provide feedback on clarity and mathematical accuracy.

###### Gallery Walk and Presentation

1. Display all artworks around the classroom.
2. Organize a gallery walk where learners observe the work of classmates.
3. Encourage learners to read the accompanying explanations while examining each artwork.
4. Provide observation sheets or guide questions for recording insights during the gallery walk.
5. Invite selected learners or pairs to briefly explain their artwork to the class.
6. Encourage respectful feedback and appreciation of diverse creative approaches.

###### UNIT 3. INTRODUCTION TO MATRICES

###### I. LEARNING GOALS

| Content Standard | The learners demonstrate knowledge and understanding of fundamental concepts of matrices and basic operations. |
|---|---|
| Performance | By the end of the quarter, the learners are able to model real-world situations using matrices and matrix operations. They are also |
| Standard | able to formulate linear programming models and solve them using either the graphical or simplex method. |
| Learning | The learners… |

**Competencies** 1. illustrate matrix and its components (e.g., row, column, dimensions, and order of a matrix);

2. perform matrix operations (addition, subtraction, scalar multiplication, multiplication, transposition);
3. apply properties of matrix operations (commutative, associative, distributive, zero matrix, identity matrix, etc.);
4. represent systems of linear equations using matrices.
5. apply row operations to solve a system of linear equations.
6. define the inverse of a matrix
7. calculate the inverse of a matrix using row operations
8. use row operations to determine if a system of linear equations is independent (unique solution), dependent consistent (infinitely many solutions), or inconsistent (no solution)
9. *compute the determinant of a 2×2 and a 3×3 matrix;*
10. use the determinant to test matrix invertibility;
11. use Cramer's rule to solve an independent system of linear equations 2×2 and 3×3 matrices

The "Data Investigator" Jigsaw - this ensures every learner is responsible for the analysis of specific regions.

###### Activity B.1. EXPLORING KEY CONCEPTS

###### Understanding Key Concepts

**B. Instituting New** key concepts of matrix - definition and its components.

A matrix is a rectangular arrangement of numbers, symbols, or

###### Knowledge

expressions in rows and columns. It is used to organize data and can help solve real-life problems in mathematics, science, and other fields.

###### Components of a Matrix

- **Element - each individual number or symbol in a matrix.**
- **Rows - horizontal lines of elements.**
- **Columns - vertical lines of elements.**
- **Dimension - indicates how many rows and columns the** matrix has. The dimension of a matrix is written as i × j, where i is the number of rows and j is the number of columns.

###### Activity B.1 EXPLORING KEY CONCEPTS

This is the part of the lesson where the teacher will discuss the

**Strategy: Explicit teaching through systematic approach where**

you clearly explain the concept, models examples, and guides learners through practice before allowing them to work independently. You must ensure that learners clearly understand the definitions, structure, and operations of matrices.

###### Examples:

1) 2 × 2 Matrix = [ 1 2 ] 3 4
    - Elements: 1, 2, 3, 4
    - Rows: [1, 2], [3, 4]
    - Columns: [1, 3], [2, 4]
    - Dimension: 2 × 2 (2 rows, 2 columns)
2) 3 x 3 Matrix

| = [ 5 | 6 | 7 ] |
|---|---|---|
| 8 | 9 11 12 13 | 10 |

- Elements: 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13
- Rows: [5, 6, 7], [8, 9, 10], [11, 12, 13]
- Columns: [5, 8, 11], [6, 9, 12], [7, 10, 13]
- Dimension: 3 × 3 (3 rows, 3 columns)
3) 2 x 3 Matrix

= [ 2 7 1 ] 4 9 6

- Elements: 1, 2, 4, 6, 7, 9
- Rows: [2, 7. 1], [4, 9, 6]
- Columns: [2, 4], [7, 9], [1, 6]

-----

- Dimension: 2 × 3 (2 rows, 3 columns)

###### Option 1: Gallery Walk Instructions:

1. Walk around the classroom to observe the posted matrices. Each station has different matrices.
2. At each station, answer the following questions on your notebook.
      a. How many rows are there?
      b. How many columns are there?
      c. What are the elements of the matrix?
      d. What is the dimension of the matrix?
3. Look at the differences between the matrices. Notice how the number of rows and columns affects the total number of elements.
4. After visiting all stations, form small groups. Discuss what you noticed about rows, columns, and dimension in each matrix.
5. Present your interesting observation to the class.

###### B.2. Discussing the Concept

###### Activity B.2. DEEPENING CONCEPTUAL UNDERSTANDING

###### Operations of Matrices

###### 1. Matrix addition is performed by adding the corresponding

**elements of two matrices that have the same dimension (same**

number of rows and columns).

###### Option 2: Matrix Match Up Challenge

1. Ask learners to form small groups of 3-5 members.
2. Give each group 5 matrix cards and 5 answer cards.
3. Instruct groups to match the cards:
    - Examine each matrix card. Identify the elements, rows, and columns and determine the order.
4. Once all matches are complete, discuss within the group:
    - Why each match is correct and the difference between the matrices.
5. Ask the learners to present and explain how they identified the rows, columns, elements, and dimension.

**Tip: Encourage learners to talk through their reasoning rather**

than guessing. Monitor groups and ask guiding questions if they 12 ] get stuck. -3

###### Option 1: Guided Practice and Independent Activity

###### Part I. Guided Practice

1. Group learners in pairs or small groups (3-4 members).
2. Provide matrices and a step-by-step guide for operations.
3. Guide learners through problems.
    - Highlight placement of elements in addition, multiplication, or transpose.
    - Encourage discussion within groups before writing final answers.
4. Walk around to clarify misconceptions and provide hints. Emphasize showing complete solutions for each operation.

###### Part II. Independent Learning

1. Instruct learners to work individually.
2. Remind them to:
    - Check matrix dimensions before performing any operation.
    - Apply the appropriate operation.
    - Show complete solutions and final answers in their notebooks.
3. Walk around to observe problem-solving and offer hints if necessary. Encourage learners to verify their results and cross-check intermediate steps.
4. Review selected problems as a class after completion, highlighting common errors and correct methods.

| Activity C.3: Assessing | Learning Outcomes |

Before facilitating this activity, ensure that all learners have a copy of the LAS. This part of the lesson is designed to measure how well learners have understood the key concepts of matrices and their operations. Learners will demonstrate their ability to:

1. Identify the components of a matrix (rows, columns, elements, dimension).
2. Perform matrix operations such as addition, subtraction, multiplication, scalar multiplication, and transpose.

***C.4. Additional Activities (For the complete instructions for the learners, please refer to the LAS.)***

###### Activity C.4: Extending and Reinforcing Learning

Before facilitating this activity, ensure that all learners have a copy of the LAS.

###### Remedial Activity: To facilitate this activity:

1. Identify learners who struggle with matrix concepts based on previous activities, quizzes, or observations.
2. Tell learners: "This activity will help you practice matrices step by step so you can understand and perform operations more confidently."
3. Depending on their level, learners can work individually or in pairs to receive guidance while solving problems.

-----

4. Provide Simplified Problems:
      - Start with small matrices.
      - Focus on identifying rows, columns, and elements, determining the dimension of a matrix and performing basic operations (addition, subtraction, scalar multiplication, transpose).
5. Solve one problem together step by step on the board. Emphasize checking dimensions and showing all steps clearly.
6. Monitor and Support: Circulate around the room, giving hints and guiding questions instead of giving answers.
7. After learners complete the activity, review selected problems together. Highlight common mistakes and clarify key concepts.

###### Enrichment activity

1. Identify learners who demonstrated mastery in guided and independent practice to participate in enrichment tasks.
2. Tell learners: "These activities will challenge you to apply your knowledge of matrices in more complex or real-life scenarios. The goal is to think critically and explore new ways to use matrices."
3. Depending on the activity, learners can work individually or in small groups to promote discussion and collaboration.
4. Provide advanced or more challenging problems.
5. Encourage exploration and creativity. Encourage learners to justify each step and explain their reasoning.
6. Have learners present interesting findings or solutions to the class.

**III. CONTENT Lesson 3.2. PROPERTIES OF MATRIX OPERATIONS IV. OBJECTIVES At the end of the lesson, the learners are expected to:**

1. **identify and describe the properties of matrix operations such as commutative, associative, distributive, identity, and** **zero matrix properties;**
2. **apply the properties of matrix operations (commutative, associative, distributive, zero matrix, identity matrix, etc.) to** **solve matrix expressions correctly; and**
3. **appreciate the importance of mathematical rules in ensuring accuracy when performing matrix operations.**

Imagine you are managing two small delivery services. Each matrix represents the number of packages delivered to different neighborhoods over two days. You want to combine, scale, or adjust these numbers efficiently. How can you be sure your calculations are correct every time? Observe the matrices displayed on the board or on your worksheet:

= [ 2 4 ], = [ 1 0 ], = [ 0 0 ]

3 5 0 1 0 0

###### Instructions:

1. Perform the following operations in pairs/ small groups.

| • | Add + | and + |
|---|---|---|
| • • | Multiply | × and × Multiply 2 × ( +) |

2. Answer the processing questions.
3. Share observations with the class.
1. Start with a story: "You need to combine deliveries from two branches. What happens if we don't follow proper rules?"
2. Guide discovery: Allow learners to perform operations and observe patterns. Ask:
      - Does the order matter for addition?
      - Which matrix doesn't change the result when added?
      - Which matrix doesn't change the result when multiplied?
3. After learners notice patterns, formally name the properties:
      - Commutative property of addition
      - Associative property
      - Distributive property
      - Zero matrix property
      - Identity matrix property
4. Emphasize that knowing these properties helps simplify calculations and avoid mistakes in real-life data handling.

###### Processing Questions:

1. Do the results change if the order of addition or multiplication changes?
2. What happens when you multiply a matrix by zero or the identity matrix?
3. What patterns or rules do you notice?

###### Option 2: Collaborative Activity

1. Tell the learners to form groups and instruct them to assign a role each member.
2. Give each group the problems.
3. Walk around to support and answer questions.
4. Let the learners present their work. Encourage other learners to ask questions.
5. Check all the answers to be presented.

###### Part I. Properties of Matrix Addition

1. Introduce the real-life scenario: "How can café sales data be organized using matrices?"
2. Emphasize that order matters in addition.
3. Guide learners to perform operations carefully step by step, especially for matrix addition.
4. Ask questions:
    - "What happens when we add by identity or zero?"
    - "Does adding another matrix change the result?"
5. Highlight commutative, identity, zero, and distributive properties.

###### Part II. Properties of Matrix Multiplication

1. Introduce the real-life scenario: "How does changing oven schedules affect total production?"
2. Emphasize that order matters in multiplication, unlike addition.
3. Guide learners to perform operations carefully step by step, especially for matrix products.
4. Ask questions:
    - "What happens when we multiply by identity or zero?"
    - "Does switching order change the result?"
5. Highlight non-commutative, identity, zero, and distributive properties.

###### Part III. Properties of Matrix Transpose

Begin with a question: "How can flipping rows and columns help us analyze data differently?"

1. Guide learners through each step carefully, highlighting how transpose affects rows and columns.
2. Encourage learners to check their work to see the patterns in properties.
3. Summarize all key transpose properties on the board:

| • | ( | ) = |
|---|---|---|
| • | ( +) = | + |
| • | ( | ) = |
| • | ( | ) = |

4. Analyze and interpret the results.
5. Present your findings clearly and justify which matrix properties were used.

###### Processing Questions:

1. Why does matrix addition satisfy the commutative property, but matrix multiplication does not always?
2. If a zero matrix is added to matrix A, what real-life meaning does it represent?
3. How does the distributive property help simplify large data computations?
4. In what situation might the identity matrix be useful in reallife data systems?
5. What would happen if matrix multiplication were commutative in real life?

Before facilitating this activity, ensure that all learners have a copy of the LAS.

In this stage, learners demonstrate their understanding of matrix operations and their properties through this activity. They are evaluated on their ability to correctly apply, verify, and explain properties like commutative, associative, distributive, zero matrix, and identity matrix properties. The facilitator uses the results to determine mastery, identify learning gaps, and provide appropriate feedback or remediation.

###### C.4. Additional Activities

###### Activity C.4. EXTENDING AND REINFORCING LEARNING

Before facilitating this activity, ensure that all learners have a copy of the LAS.

###### Remedial Activity

1. Begin with a brief review of matrix operations before giving the activity.
2. Guide learners to focus on patterns, not only computation.
3. Provide step-by-step scaffolding if learners struggle with calculations.
4. Encourage learners to compare results (e.g., + vs. + ).
5. After the activity, ask learners to state the property in their own words.
6. Reinforce the lesson using visual examples or concept maps.

###### Enrichment Activity

1. Present this activity as an extension task for advanced learners who quickly master the basic concepts.
2. Encourage LEARNERs to explain their reasoning, not only compute answers.
3. Ask probing questions such as:
    a. *Why does the property hold?*
    b. *Can this property apply to all matrices?*
    c. *How can this concept be used in real-life situations?*
4. Allow pair or group discussion to promote deeper mathematical thinking.
5. Conclude by asking learners to summarize the matrix properties they applied.

**III. CONTENT Lesson 3.3. SOLVING SYSTEMS OF LINEAR EQUATIONS USING MATRICES AND ROW OPERATIONS**

###### At the end of this lesson the learners are expected to:

###### 1. represent a system of linear equations as an augmented matrix accurately;

**IV. OBJECTIVES 2. illustrate and perform all basic matrix row operations: row swapping, scalar multiplication, and row addition; and**

3. **apply row operations to reduce matrices to their reduced row echelon form (RREF) and solve systems of linear** **equations.** **V. PROCEDURES ACTIVITIES ANNOTATION** **A. Activating Prior** ***A.1. Eliciting Prior Knowledge (15 mins)*** **Knowledge Activity A.1. LEVELING LEARNERS READINESS Activity A.1. LEVELING LEARNERS READINESS**

| Instruction: Find a pair and then solve the following system of linear equations. { + = 5 2 + 3 = 11 { + + = 6 2 +3 + = 13 + 3 +2 = 11 Processing Questions: 1. How did you solve these systems? 2. How long did it take to solve each system? 3. What are the challenges that you encountered as the system increases its size? | The primary goal of this activity is to highlight the limitations of traditional algebraic methods (substitution and elimination) when applied to larger systems. By starting with a simple 2 x 2 and moving to a 3 x 3, learners will personally experience the increase in complexity, "bookkeeping" errors, and time required. This provides the pedagogical "hook" for matrices, positioning them not as a "new difficult topic," but as a tool for organization and efficiency. Encourage learners to work in pairs to solve both systems as quickly as possible using any method they remember from previous years. Walk around and notice which learners struggle with the 3 x 3 system. Many will likely get lost in the multiple steps of elimination or make a sign error. Use the Processing Questions to facilitate a class discussion. ● For Question 1: Most will say "elimination." ● For Question 3: Highlight words like "messy," "confusing," or "hard to keep track of variables." Use their struggle as a bridge: "What if I told you there is a way to solve these by stripping away the letters and just manipulating the numbers in a grid? That is what matrices are for." |
|---|---|
| A.2. Establishing the Purpose of the Lesson (20 mins) |  |
| Activity A.2. APPRECIATING LESSON RELEVANCE Real-life examples of applying system of linear equations: 1. Business: Determining the number of products needed to meet revenue or resource constraints. The Scenario: A bakery sells Cupcakes for P 3.00 and Cookies for P2.00. Goal 1: They want to make exactly 50 items total today. Goal 2: They need to earn exactly P120 to cover their rent. The System: Let be the number of cupcakes and be the number of cookies. 1. + = 50 (Total items constraint) 2. 3 + 2 = 120 (Revenue constraint) | Activity A.2. APPRECIATING LESSON RELEVANCE Explain that matrices provide a systematic, visual, and structured approach to representing and solving systems of linear equations. This serves as the Motivational Framework. After the struggle of the "Pair Activity," LEARNERs might feel that 3x3 systems are simply "more work." By citing Business, Engineering, and Computer Science, you are validating the utility of matrices in solving system of linear equations in professional fields. The teacher can state that: "In our previous activity, we saw that as the number of variables increased, the chance of making an error also increases. In the professional world, we deal with hundreds of variables at once." |

EXPLORING KEY CONCEPTS A system of linear equations can be represented by three matrices defined as follows: = Where: ● Matrix A - contains all the coefficients of the system ● Matrix X - contains all the variables that is in a form of a column matrix ● Matrix B - is also a column matrix that includes all the values at the right side of the equation. Example 1: 2 x 2 System Representation { + = 5 2 + 3 = 11 Matrix Representation = [1 1 2 3 ] , = [ ] , = [5 11 ] Example 2: 3 x 3 System Representation { + + = 6 2 +3 + = 13 + 3 +2 = 11 | Activity B.1. EXPLORING KEY CONCEPTS The goal of this section is pattern recognition. Learners often struggle with the transition because they view the and as essential anchors. By presenting these examples, you are teaching them that the position of a number in a matrix replaces the label of the variable. It is important to emphasize that you should first arrange the variables within the system properly. For the Matrix A, it includes all the coefficients of the system of linear equations. The first column includes the coefficient of the first variable, the second column includes all the coefficients of the second variable and so on. For Matrix X, it is a column matrix that includes all the variables involved within the system of linear equations. For Matrix B, it includes all the values at the right side of the equation. |

| B.2. Discussing the Concept |  |
| Activity B.2. DEEPENING CONCEPTUAL UNDERSTANDING Matrix Row Operations A row operation is an action applied to the rows of a matrix that transforms it without changing the solution set of the corresponding system of linear equations. ● Row Swapping ( ↔ ) - exchanging two rows of a matrix [1 0 0 0 1 0 0 0 1 ] [0 1 0 1 0 0 0 0 1 ] 1 ↔ 2 ● Row Multiplication ( ) - multiplying a non-zero scalar to all the elements within the row [2 0 0 0 1 0 0 0 1 ] [1 0 0 0 1 0 0 0 1 ] 2 1 → 1 ● Row Addition ( + → ) - Add a scalar multiple of one row to another row, and replace the later row with that sum [1 0 0 0 1 0 0 0 1 ] [1 0 0 0 1 0 3 0 1 ] 3 1 + 3 → 3 Elementary Matrix An elementary matrix is a matrix formed by applying exactly one row operation on identity matrix. | Activity B.2. DEEPENING CONCEPTUAL UNDERSTANDING Before diving into the operations, address the "why". Learners often view these as arbitrary rules. Compare a matrix to a system of equations. Explain that swapping two equations or multiplying an entire equation by 2 doesn't change the solution. Emphasize that they aren't just changing numbers; they are "cleaning up" the matrix to reveal the answers. Row Swapping is essential for getting a non-zero number (ideally a 1) into the "pivot" position (the diagonal). Note that a common mistake is when learners often forget to swap the entire row. Row Multiplication is our tool for creating "1s." If a row starts with a 5, multiply by 1/5 to get that leading 1. Remind LEARNERs that the scalar () cannot be zero. Ask them: "What happens to our information if we multiply an equation by 0?" (It disappears). |

-----

#### Applying Sequence of Row Operations

#### Direction: Determine the sequence of row operations applied to the identity matrix (3) to obtain the following matrices.

- 3 2 + 1 → 1
- 5 3 + 1 → 1
2. [1 0 3 0 2 0 0 0 1 ]

Row Addition is considered as the powerhouse operation. It's how we create "0s" above and below our pivots. It is a combination of Scalar Multiplication and Addition.

Note: Encourage learners to use scratch papers when dealing with matrices especially in operations.

#### Elementary Matrix

Start the lesson by challenging their understanding of . Tell them that if is the "identity," then an Elementary Matrix () is a "Matrix with a Memory."

Direct them to look at each example on the worksheet and treat it like a "Spot the Difference" puzzle. Because they already know the 1s belong on the diagonal and 0s belong everywhere else, any deviation they see is a direct clue to the operation performed.

#### Guide your learners to categorize the matrices based on the specific action done to the Identity Matrix.

#### into a different number (as seen in Problems 3 and 4), instruct them to label this as Row Multiplication.

- If a 0 has turned into a non-zero constant (as seen in

#### Problems 5 and 6), they must recognize this as Row Addition.

#### Reduced Row Echelon Form (RREF)

A matrix is in reduced row echelon form if the entries along the main diagonal are all 1 and the rest of the entries are all

0. Sequence of row operations are applied to reduce a matrix to its reduced row echelon form.

#### Example: Reduce the following matrices.

1. [1 1 1 - 1 ]

#### Solution:

2. [2 3 4 5 ]

#### Solution:

#### [2 3 4 5 ] - 2 1 + 2 → 2 [2 3 0 - 1 ] 3 2 + 1 → 1 1 [2 0 0 - 1 ] 1 → 1

To effectively deliver this lesson on Sequences of Row Operations, you must transition your learners from seeing a matrix as a single "snapshot" of a rule to viewing it as the result of a mathematical narrative.

Since your learners are already comfortable with individual elementary matrices, direct them to approach these complex matrices as "crime scenes" where multiple actions have occurred. Instruct them to perform a systematic Inventory of

Direct the learners toward the realization that each non-zero entry in a "zero-slot" represents a separate row addition operation that must be accounted for in the sequence.

#### Reduced Row Echelon Form

You need to frame this not as a math problem, but as a strategic clean - up operation. Start by reminding your learners that while they have the three options of row operations, the real challenge is the sequence.

Direct them to treat the matrix like a puzzle where the goal is to reveal the Identity Matrix hidden inside. You must be firm about the order of operations here. Tell them to work column by column, from left to right. If they try to fix a number in the third column before the first is finished, they'll end up in a loop situation where they keep breaking the zeros they just created.

As you walk them through the worked examples from the slides, direct their attention to the "Pivot Strategy." Instruct them to always get their 1 first. Once they have a leading 1 in the top-left corner, emphasize that this 1 is now their tool to create zeros in the rest of that column.

Note: Watch for learners who try to do mental math here.

#### Insist them to write the "scratch row" off to the side. of a system of Key Delivery Checkpoints:

- Always secure a 1 in the diagonal before attempting to

create 0s in that column.

- Once a column looks like the identity (1 on the

#### diagonal, 0s elsewhere), it is locked. Tell them to never use a row operation that would change those established values.

#### Solving Systems of Linear Equation through Matrices

Solving a system of linear equations using matrices and reduced row echelon form (RREF) is the process of representing the system as an augmented matrix and applying row operations to transform the matrix into reduced row echelon form, from which the values of the variables can be determined directly.

#### [|] is not just a line, it is a mathematical "equal sign" that right.

| Knowledge and Skills | Activity C.1. MAKING REAL-WORLD CONNECTION Instruction: Analyze each problem. Translate each situations into systems of linear equation and find its solution using matrices. Jeepney, Tricycle, and Bus Fare A commuter traveling to work in Manila used three types of transportation: jeepney, tricycle, and bus. ● On Monday, the commuter paid ₱115 for 2 jeepney rides, 1 tricycle ride, and 1 bus ride. ● On Tuesday, the commuter paid ₱150 for 1 jeepney ride, 2 tricycle rides, and 2 bus ride. ● On Wednesday, the commuter paid ₱115 for 3 jeepney ride, 1 tricycle ride, and 1 bus ride. What is the fare for each type of transportation? Rice, Corn, and Sugar Purchase A customer bought goods from a market. | Activity C.1. MAKING REAL-WORLD CONNECTION Start by emphasizing that the most critical phase of this activity is the translation from sentences to systems of linear equations. Direct the teacher to walk the learners through the "Jeepney, Tricycle, and Bus" scenario, instructing them to first explicitly define their variables, such as letting represent the jeepney fare, the tricycle fare, and the bus fare. As LEARNERs begin the heavy lifting of reducing these 3 × 3 matrices into Reduced Row Echelon Form (RREF), you must direct them to maintain extreme procedural discipline to avoid errors and loops in solution. Instruct them to use the first row as their anchor to create zeros in the first column, specifically aiming to simplify the large totals. Finally, direct the learners to interpret their final matrix not just as numbers, but as the actual prices they were seeking. |

-----

| ● The customer paid ₱175 for 1 kg of rice, 2 kg of corn, and 1 kg of sugar. ● Another customer paid ₱265 for 2 kg of rice, 1 kg of corn, and 2 kg of sugar. ● A third customer paid ₱200 for 1 kg of rice, 1 kg of corn, and 2 kg of sugar. Find the price per kilogram of each product. Filipino Street Food Sales During a local event in a plaza, a vendor sold fish balls, kikiam, and squid balls. ● One group paid ₱120 for 2 fish balls, 1 kikiam, and 3 squid balls. ● Another group paid ₱125 for 1 fish ball, 3 kikiam, and 2 squid balls. ● A third group paid ₱110 for 3 fish balls, 2 kikiam, and 1 squid balls. Determine the price of one order of each product. | Processing Questions: 1. When translating a real-world scenario into a system of equations, how do you determine which pieces of information become the coefficients in Matrix and which become the constants in Matrix ? Describe how you ensure that each row and column in your augmented matrix represents a consistent category from the story. 2. After reaching the Reduced Row Echelon Form (RREF), how do you verify that the numerical values in the final column are logically sound within the context of the original problem? Why is it important to interpret these numbers back into their real- world labels (like price, weight, or distance) rather than just leaving them as variables , , and ? |

| Activity C.2. WRAPPING UP THE LESSON Steps Summary: 1. Represent system as augmented matrix. 2. Apply row operations to reach RREF. 3. Interpret solution from the RREF matrix. Questions: 1. Why is RREF more efficient than substitution or elimination for large systems? 2. How can this method save time in real-world applications like business, engineering, or scheduling? 3. What value does mastering row operations add to logical problem solving? | Activity C.2. WRAPPING UP THE LESSON Direct your learners to see the summary of steps, representing the system, applying operations, and interpreting the RREF, as a universal logical workflow for solving systems of linear equation using matrices. You should explicitly instruct them that the true value of mastering these matrices isn't just in the arithmetic, but in the efficiency, it provides over traditional substitution or elimination when dealing with the large, multi-variable datasets they will encounter in real - life. Direct the teacher to use the "Steps Summary" as a diagnostic checklist; if a learner is struggling, instruct them to pinpoint exactly which of the three stages is breaking down. |

**III. CONTENT Lesson 3.4. MATRIX INVERSES AND LINEAR CLASSIFICATIONS**

###### At the end of this lesson the learners are expected to:

1. define the inverse of a square matrix and state the identity property - = .;

###### IV. OBJECTIVES

2. **calculate the inverse of matrices using the augmented matrix method [|] through elementary row operations; and**
3. **classify systems of linear equations as independent, dependent consistent, or inconsistent based on the results of** **row reduction and the inverse of the matrix.** **V. Direct their attention to the simple equation 3 = 12 and ask them to physically "undo" the multiplication. You want to emphasize that division is simply the inverse operation of multiplication; it's the tool we use to neutralize the 3 and isolate x. Once they've acknowledged that we divide both sides to maintain a balance, challenge them with a "what if" scenario: what if we had two variables and two equations? Show them the system of equations and then reveal the Matrix Representation. It is vital here that you explain that = isn't just a new way to write the problem, it's a way to treat an entire system of equations as if it were a single, simple algebraic sentence. Now, lead them into the "Think-Pair-Share" segment by drawing a direct parallel between the two images. Ask them, "If we used division to isolate in the first problem, what is the 'matrix version' of division?" Guide them to see that since we can't "divide" matrices in the -1 traditional sense, we use the Inverse Matrix instead. Explain that -1 is the mathematical equivalent of an "undo" button. |

When you present these examples to your learners, your goal is to transition them from mechanical calculation to a discovery about the nature of inverse matrices.

Don't give away the secret immediately; instead, let them work through the multiplication of the first pair. As they calculate the result for matrices and , you should circulate and watch for the moment they realize the product is the Identity Matrix. Use this as a pivot point to ask them what it feels like to multiply these specific numbers together. You want them to see that the "1" of the field of matrices is this specific diagonal pattern of ones and zeros.

Connect this back to the broader goal of solving systems. You need to make sure they understand that finding this pair of matrices is the most critical step in isolating variables. Remind them that just as they used division in simple algebra to reach the number 1, they are using matrix multiplication here to reach the Identity Matrix. If they can grasp that = means is the inverse of , they are ready to proceed.

###### Independent System (Unique Solution)

{ x + y+ z = 6 2x - 3y +z = 3

+ 2 - 2 = 3 Augmented Matrix:

Since the coefficient matrix within the augmented matrix is reduced to its identity, therefore the system is Independent or has

a unique solution.

Transition immediately to the scenario where the inverse does not exist, which occurs when a row of zeros appears on the left side during reduction. You must train your LEARNERs to look past the left-hand side and analyze the resulting constant on the righthand side of that specific row.

Explain that this is where the classification splits into two distinct paths. If the reduction results in a row of all zeros across both sides (0 = 0), the system is Dependent Consistent, meaning the equations are redundant and there are infinitely many solutions. But, if the reduction produces a row of zeros on the left but a nonzero constant on the right (0 = , ℎ - ), the system is Inconsistent, meaning the planes or lines never intersect and no solution exists.

###### Dependent Consistent System (Infinitely Many Solutions)

{ x + y+ z = 3 2x + 2y+ 2z = 6 - + = 1 Augmented Matrix:

| [1 | 1 | 1 \|3 ] |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | 2 1 -1 1 1 | 2 6 |

Reduced Augmented Matrix:

[1 1 1 |3 ] 0 1 0 1 0 0 0 0 Since there is a row that has all zero entries (0 = 0), then the system of linear equation is dependent consistent or has infinitely many solutions.

###### Inconsistent System (No Solution)

{ x + y+ z = 4 2x + y+ z = 6

+ 2 + = 7 Augmented Matrix:

-----

[1 1 1 |4 ] 2 1 1 6 1 2 1 7 Reduced Augmented Matrix:

[1 1 1 | 4 ] 0 -1 -1 -2 0 0 0 1 Since there is a row that has all zero entries on the left and mapped to a non - zero value on the right (0 = , ℎ - ), then the system of linear equation is an inconsistent system or has no solutions.

***B.3. Developing Mastery (For the complete instructions for the learners, please refer to the LAS.)***

**Activity B.3. PRACTICING LEARNED SKILLS: INVESTIGATIVE TABLE INVOLVING MATRICES**

Before facilitating this activity, ensure that all learners have a copy of the LAS.

Your primary objective is to direct their attention to the structural relationship between matrix invertibility and system consistency. Instruct them to begin by representing each system as an augmented matrix [|]. You must emphasize that their first goal is to determine the existence of an inverse by attempting to transform the left side into the Identity matrix.

If they achieve an identity matrix, instruct them to identify the system as Independent and extract the unique solution from the righthand column. However, if they encounter a row of zeros on the left, tell them they must analyze the corresponding value on the right. If that value is zero, they should classify the system as Dependent Consistent because the statement implies infinite solutions. If the value is non-zero, they must classify the system as Inconsistent because a statement like 0 = 8 is a contradiction, proving no solution exists.

**Processing Questions:** To deliver this activity effectively, you must focus the learners'

| 2. What if a row of zeroes appear? Is it still possible that a matrix is still invertible? Based on the activities, we can assume that: 1. The Matrix is Invertible (Inverse exists) if we can successfully create a diagonal of 1's on the left side. 2. The Matrix is Non-Invertible (No inverse) if any row on the left side reduces to all zeros. • Classification of Matrices based on its Inverse 1. Can you classify a system of linear equations: Independent, Dependent Consistent, and Inconsistent based on its inverse? How and what are the constraints to consider? System Logic Resulting Row Inverse Classification Check Status Independent Valid Diagonal of Exists 1's A row of zero No Inverse Dependent 0 = 0 (Always appears and True) is mapped to zero Inconsistent 0 = c A row of zero No Inverse (Impossi appears and ble) mapped to non - zero scalar Processing Questions: 1. If you find a row of zeros on the left side, do you even need to try and find the inverse anymore? 2. Can a system be Inconsistent (No Solution) if the inverse exists? 3. Why does 0 = mean 'No Solution' while 0 = 0 means 'Infinite Solutions'? | Instruct your LEARNERs to begin by translating each system into an augmented matrix and immediately identify the diagonal elements. Direct them to perform row operations with the singular goal of reaching the identity matrix on the left side. You should emphasize that the moment a row of zeros appears on the left side of the vertical bar, the matrix is technically non-invertible, and they must stop searching for an inverse. At this juncture, direct them to shift their analysis to the constant on the right side of that zeroed row to determine the system's consistency. Explain to the class that if the left-side zero row corresponds to a zero on the right, they have encountered a "true" identity statement, which characterizes a Dependent Consistent system with infinite solutions. Conversely, if the zero row corresponds to a non-zero constant, they must recognize this as a mathematical contradiction, which identifies the system as Inconsistent with no possible solution. You must ensure that they do not equate "No Inverse" exclusively with "No Solution"; rather, they must use the constant as the definitive tie-breaker. By enforcing this binary check, first for invertibility on the left, then for logic on the right, you provide them with a concrete, repeatable method for system classification that relies on mathematical evidence rather than intuition. |

To evaluate learner mastery, you must direct them to perform a full system audit on three distinct cases. Instruct your LEARNERs that they are not merely solving for , , and , but are diagnosing the mathematical structure of each system.

Direct them to prioritize the "Left Side" of the augmented matrix to determine invertibility. You must emphasize that a row of zeros on the left side is the definitive evidence of a non-invertible matrix. Once they identify a non-invertible state, direct them to use the "Right Side" constant to decide if the system is logically consistent (Dependent) or logically impossible (Inconsistent). Ensure they articulate the "Why" for each classification using the logic checks to prove they understand the underlying principles of the lesson.

When addressing struggling learners, you must strip away the complexity of 3x3 calculations and focus entirely on visual pattern recognition. Direct your learners to look at the "Bottom Row" of the matrix as a "Truth Detector." Instruct them that they do not need to solve the entire matrix to understand the system's nature; they only need to interpret the final row. Use a face-to-face, direct approach: "Ignore the top two rows for a moment. Look at the last row. Does it make sense, is it a repeat, or is it a lie?" Your goal is to build their confidence in identifying Invertibility and Classification by looking at the relationship between the zero and the constant.

###### Activity C.1. MAKING REAL-WORLD CONNECTION

###### Visual Examples of Real-Life Applications of Matrices and Determinants

###### 1. Traffic Mapping (Road Networks

###### Explanation for Learners:

Traffic systems in cities can be represented using matrices. Each intersection and road connection can be organized in rows and columns. Traffic engineers use matrices to analyze traffic flow, optimize routes, and reduce congestion.

###### Example teacher statement:

"Each intersection can be represented as a point, and the connections between them can be organized in a matrix to study traffic flow."

###### 2. Seating Arrangements (Rows and Columns)

###### Explanation for learners:

Seating arrangements in classrooms, theaters, or exam halls

-----

Ma, X., Dai, Z., He, Z., Na, J., Wang, Y., & Wang, Y. (2017). *Learning Traffic as Images: A Deep Convolutional Neural Network* *for Large-Scale Transportation Network Speed Prediction. Sensors.*

###### 2. Seating Arrangements (Rows and Columns)

are naturally arranged in rows and columns, which form a matrix structure.

###### Example teacher statement:

"If we label rows and columns, every seat has a position like

![](img_p162_2.png)

![](img_p162_1.png)

![](img_p162_3.png)

(row, column). That is exactly how matrices are organized."

###### Explanation:

Seating charts visually represent the arrangement of chairs or desks in rows and columns, similar to the structure of a matrix.

###### Additional explanation:

A classroom seating layout can also be treated like a twodimensional array or matrix, where each seat is identified by its row and column position

###### Reference / Source:

- EdrawSoft. Seating Chart Examples and Templates.

###### 3. Business Data

###### 3. Business Data Tables

###### Explanation for Learners:

Companies organize information like sales, expenses, and inventory in tables that look like matrices. Each row and column contains specific data used for analysis and decision-making.

###### Example teacher statement:

"Spreadsheets in business work very much like matrices because they store data in rows and columns."

###### 4. Digital Images (Pixels)

###### Explanation for Learners:

Digital images are made of tiny squares called pixels arranged in rows and columns. Each pixel stores color values, forming a matrix of numbers that computers

![](img_p162_5.png)

process to display images.

![](img_p162_4.png)

###### Example teacher statement:

"When you zoom into a digital image, you will see tiny squares. These squares form a matrix that computers use to display pictures."

###### Explanation:

Digital images are represented as matrices where each element corresponds to a pixel value, which stores color or intensity information Reference / Source: Math Warehouse. Matrix Definition and Examples.

-----

###### 4. Digital Images (Pixels)

![](img_p163_2.png)

![](img_p163_1.png)

Digital images are represented as matrices where each element corresponds to a pixel value, which stores color or intensity information Reference / Source:

| Activity A.2. APPRECIATING LESSON RELEVANCE: WHAT DOES A 3×3 DETERMINANT REPRESENT? | Activity A.2. APPRECIATING LESSON RELEVANCE: WHAT DOES A 3×3 DETERMINANT REPRESENT? |

###### Instructions:

1. Think back to what you learned about 2×2 determinants and area.
2. Discuss with your group:
    - "If area changes in 2D, what changes in 3D?"
    - "How might a 3×3 matrix describe that change?"
3. Write your group's answer on the board or share aloud.
4. Listen as your teacher explains how determinants extend from 2D to 3D
1. Recall prior concept: Ask: "If a 2×2 determinant tells us how *area is scaled, what do you think a 3×3 determinant tells us?"* *Annotation: Encourage learners to reason from geometric* intuition rather than formulas.
2. Guide discussion:
    a. Let learners share ideas (e.g., "space," "volume," "3D shape").
    b. Confirm that the determinant of a 3×3 matrix represents *volume scaling in three-dimensional space.*
3. Connect to lesson goal: Say: "Today, we'll extend determinants to 3×3 matrices using two methods-expansion and shortcut rules. These help us find how transformations affect volume."
4. Visual reinforcement (optional): Show a cube or rectangular prism and explain that changing its dimensions can be modeled by a 3×3 matrix-its determinant tells how the volume changes.

**Activity B.2. DEEPENING CONCEPTUAL UNDERSTANDING Activity B.2. DEEPENING CONCEPTUAL UNDERSTANDING**

**Methods on Determining of Determinants of a 3 X 3 Matrices** 1. Present the two methods of finding the determinant of 3x3 Method 1: matrices. **Expansion by Minors and Cofactors (The Universal Method)** 2. Guide the learners in to find the determinant of the matrix using

1. Introduce the concept of a minor: Explain that for every element the two methods. in a matrix, there is a "minor," which is the determinant of the

-----

smaller matrix left over when you cross out that element's row

![](img_p167_3.png)

and column.

2. Explain cofactors: Introduce the concept that each minor is multiplied by a "cofactor," which is either +1 or -1, depending on the element's position. Display the alternating sign grid for a 3x3 matrix:

###### Cofactor Expansions

A recursive formula must have a starting point. For cofactor expansions, the starting point is the case of matrices. The definition of determinant directly implies that 1x1 matrices. To describe cofactor expansions, we need to introduce some notation.

det(a) = a

![](img_p167_2.png)

###### Definition : Minor and Cofactor

Let be an nxn matrix.

1. The (i.j) minor, denoted is **Aij,** obtained from A by deleting the ith row and the jth column.
2. The (i.j) cofactor Cij is defined in terms of the minor by

###### Cij = (-1)i+j det(Aij)

Note that the signs of the cofactors follow a "checkerboard

pattern." Namely, (-1)i+j is pictured in this matrix:

[+ - + - + - + - + ]+ - + - + - + - +

**Method 2: Sarrus' Rule (The Shortcut Method for 3x3)**

3. Explain the setup: Show learners how to rewrite the first two columns of the matrix to the right of the original matrix.

    a. Which method was faster?
    b. Which method helped you understand the process better?
    c. Did both groups get the same determinant value?
4. Conclude the activity bay saying: "Both methods lead to the same result, but each has its own advantage. Sarrus' Rule is quick and visual, while expansion by minors ensures accuracy and deeper understanding."

###### Instructions:

1. Review the two methods Briefly recap Sarrus' Rule and Cofactor Expansion using one simple example per method.
2. Present two 3×3 matrices Choose one "clean" matrix suitable for Sarrus' Rule and one more complex matrix ideal for Cofactor Expansion.
3. Guide learners in comparison Ask learners to examine the matrices and discuss which method they think will work better and why.
4. Facilitate computation Allow learners to compute determinants using the method they chose first, then require them to verify using the other method.
5. Lead a reflection discussion Ask questions such as:
  a. "Which method was faster for you?"
  b. "Which method felt more reliable or less confusing?"
  c. "When might accuracy matter more than speed?"
6. Summarize the core idea Emphasize that Sarrus is fast but limited, while Cofactor

***C.4. Additional Activities (For the complete instructions for the learners, please refer to the LAS.)***

**Activity C.4. EXTENDING AND REINFORCING LEARNING: CHOOSE THE BEST METHOD**

Before facilitating this activity, ensure that all learners have a copy of the LAS.

1. Organize learners into small groups (3-4 members).
2. Provide each group with two 3×3 matrices that differ in structure (e.g., presence of zeros, repeated entries).
3. Explain that learners must:

-----

a. Decide which method (Sarrus' Rule or Cofactor Expansion) is more appropriate for each matrix.
b. Justify their choice before computing.
4. Emphasize that method selection and reasoning are as important as the final answer.
5. Circulate around the classroom to:
a. Guide group discussions
b. Ask probing questions
c. Correct misconceptions when necessary
6. Call on selected groups for the presentation.
7. Summarize key takeaways on the board regarding efficient method selection.

###### A.2. Establishing the Purpose of the Lesson

**Activity A. 2. APPRECIATING LESSON RELEVANCE Activity A. 2. APPRECIATING LESSON RELEVANCE**

###### Instructions:

1. Observe the following everyday actions:
    a. Mixing two colors of paint.
    b. Encrypting a message using a code.
    c. Rotating or resizing an image on a computer.
    d. Solving a system of equations to find unknowns.
2. Discuss in groups: Which of these actions can be undone to recover the original state? Which cannot?
3. Classify each situation as reversible or non-reversible and explain your reasoning.
4. Share your group's insights with the class.
5. Reflect: What makes some processes reversible while others are not? How might mathematics help us describe this idea?
1. Encourage learners to connect "reversibility" with mathematical operations they already know (e.g., addition/subtraction, multiplication/division).
2. Use their examples to introduce the idea that some matrices allow reversal while others do not-without naming the concept yet.
3. Conclude by saying: "In our next activity, we'll explore how to determine whether a matrix can be reversed using a special property."

###### Processing Questions:

1. What patterns do you notice among reversible processes?
2. How can we represent such processes mathematically?
3. What might prevent a process from being reversed?

###### Real-Life Scenarios (Learners Pick One)

###### Scenario 1. Traffic Flow & Route Prediction

A city uses a 3×3 travel-time matrix to estimate commuter flow between three major intersections. If the determinant is non-zero, the system can predict unique traffic adjustments.

-----

If it is zero, the model collapses and traffic behavior cannot be uniquely predicted. Matrix: = [ 0 12 18 ]

10 0 20 15 14 0

**Scenario 2. Image Transformations (Computer Graphics)**

A 3×3 transformation matrix is used to rotate and scale a digital object. A non-invertible matrix means the transformation cannot be reversed, distorting the image permanently.

###### Matrix:

= [ 1 0 0 ] 0 2 1 0 - 1 1

**Scenario 3. Cryptography (Encoding & Decoding Messages)**

A message is encoded using a 3×3 key matrix. To decode the message, the matrix must be invertible. If the determinant = 0 → the message cannot be recovered. Matrix:

= [ 4 7 2 ] 3 5 1 2 6 3

###### Scenario 4. Economic Forecasting

Economists analyze how GDP, inflation, and employment interact using a 3×3 matrix. If the determinant is zero, the indicators are dependent and forecasts will be unreliable. Matrix: = [ 2 1 3 ]

1 2 1 4 1 5

**Scenario 5. Robot Arm Movement (3D Orientation Matrix)**

A robot uses a 3×3 orientation matrix to move in 3D space. If the matrix is NOT invertible, the robot loses degrees of freedom and cannot reverse or correct movements. Matrix:

= [ 1 0 2 ]

3 - 1 4 2 5 1

###### B.2. Discussing the Concept

**Activity B.2. DEEPENING CONCEPTUAL UNDERSTANDING:**

###### LINEAR PROGRAMMING AND ITS COMPONENTS

###### Part 1. Linear Programming and Its Components (Variables, Objective, Constraints)

Linear programming is defined as a type of optimization problem that involves maximizing or minimizing a linear objective function, subject to linear equality and inequality constraints.

###### Key Terms:

Decision Variables - unknown quantities that need to be determined in order to achieve the optimal solution. Objective Function - a mathematical expression that represents the goal to be maximized or minimized. Constraints - linear inequalities or equations that represent the limitations or restrictions that the decision variables must satisfy.

Play a short teacher-selected video or presentation introducing Linear Programming (optional).

Ask learners to share key points they learned. The teacher may list their responses on the board.

###### Example 1: Recognizing LP Situations

A farmer wants to plant corn and beans on 10 hectares of land and maximize profit.

Learners identify:

**Activity B.2. DEEPENING CONCEPTUAL UNDERSTANDING: LINEAR PROGRAMMING AND ITS COMPONENTS**

You should avoid presenting the mathematical model immediately. Instead, facilitate a guided discovery process where learners analyze situations and gradually identify the decision variables, objective function, and constraints. This approach promotes conceptual understanding rather than procedural memorization.

Play a short video or presentation introducing Linear Programming (optional).

Ask learners to share key points they learned. You may list their responses on the board

###### Example 1. Recognizing LP Situations Processing Questions: These questions guide learners to

recognize the key components of Linear Programming (decisions, objective, and constraints), without immediately presenting the mathematical model.

Facilitate Example 2 to reinforce the identification of a Linear Programming situation after the farmer example. Ask learners to analyze the situation and identify what decisions must be made, what the goal is, and what limits the decisions. Guide learners to recognize that the bakery must decide how many brownies and cookies to produce, that the goal is to maximize profit, and that flour and time are constraints.

-----

- Decision variables: area planted to corn and area planted to beans
- Objective function: maximize profit
- Constraint: total land area is 10 hectares

###### Processing Questions

1. To identify decision variables
    a. What choices does the farmer need to make?
    b. What quantities can change in this situation?
    c. What does the farmer decide about corn and beans?
2. To identify the objective (goal)
    a. What is the farmer trying to achieve?
    b. Is the farmer trying to increase something or decrease something?
    c. What does "maximize profit" mean in this context?
3. To identify constraints (limitations)
    a. What limits the farmer's decisions?
    b. How much land is available?
    c. Can the farmer use more than 10 hectares? Why or why not?
4. To check if it is a Linear Programming situation
    a. Are the quantities involved measurable?
    b. Is there a clear goal to optimize?
    c. Are there limits or restrictions on the choices?
    d. Can this situation be represented using numbers and inequalities?
5. To deepen understanding
    a. What would happen if there were no land limit?
    b. Why do you think land is an important constraint in this problem?

###### Example 2: Business Production

A bakery produces brownies and cookies. It aims to maximize profit but has limited flour and time.

- Decision variables: number of brownies and number of cookies produced
- Objective function: maximize profit
- Constraint: limited flour and available time

-----

###### Part II. LP or Not LP? Instruction: Determine whether each scenario can be

modeled using LP.

a. Planning meals with nutritional requirements (LP)
b. Choosing clothes for a party (Not LP)
c. Scheduling workers with limited working hours (LP)
d. Allocating budget across school projects (LP)

###### Processing Questions

1. "What quantities can be measured in this situation?"
2. "Is there a clear goal to maximize or minimize?"
3. "Are there restrictions or limitations involved?"

###### Mini-Practice: Anchor the Maximization Template

A school canteen sells turon (x pieces) and banana cue (y pieces) per day. Each turon earns ₱8 profit; each banana cue earns ₱6. The canteen has at most 30 oil-units per day; turon uses 2 oil-units, banana cue uses

- Write the LP model in the four-part form (decision variables with units; objective; constraint; nonnegativity) without solving it.

###### Processing Questions:

1. Which variable letter did you use for the objective value?
2. Which inequality direction (≤ or ≥) did the phrase "at most 30 oil-units" produce?

#### 3. Is your model a maximization or a minimization, and how

does the direction word affect the optimum?

###### Part II. LP or Not LP

1. Present the situation.
2. Ask learners what quantities must be represented.
3. Assign variables based on learner suggestions.
4. Ask what the goal of the problem is.
5. Translate limitations into mathematical expressions.
6. Write each part on the board as students contribute.

Learners explain why a scenario is or isn't an LP problem.

**Mini-Practice: Anchor the Maximization Template. Big idea. This mini-practice anchors the maximization template**

(capital P for the objective, ≤ for "at most" resource constraints) before the next example broadens scope.

**Expected answers. Decision variables: x = pieces of turon per**

day; y = pieces of banana cue per day. Objective: Maximize P = 8x + 6y, where P is daily profit in pesos. Constraint: 2x + y ≤ 30 (oil-units per day). Non-negativity: x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0.

###### Possible answers to process questions. (1) P. (2) ≤. (3)

Maximization; the direction word fixes whether the optimum is the corner farthest from the origin (max) or closest to it (min), with the same constraint set giving different optimal corners.

**Anticipated misconception. Students sometimes write the**

objective as "Income = 8x + 6y" without "Maximize" and without a symbol; redirect to the form "Maximize P = (coeff)x + (coeff)y." Materials: paper and pencil. No technology required.

###### Part III. Linear Programming Model Construction

###### 1. Identify the Decision Variables

a. Decision variables are the unknown quantities that we want to determine. They represent the choices available in the problem.
b. Each variable must represent a measurable quantity.
c. Variables must be clearly and simply defined.
d. They are usually represented by x, y (or more variables if needed), but the description of what they represent is more important than the symbol.

###### Part III. Linear Programming Model Construction Probing Questions

1. For identifying variables *"What quantities change in this situation?"* *"What are we trying to determine?"*
2. For writing the objective *"What outcome do we want to achieve?"* *"How does each variable contribute to that outcome?"*
3. For identifying constraints

-----

###### 2. Write the Objective Function

The objective function expresses what we want to maximize or minimize. It is based on the goal of the problem. There are two types:

###### a. Maximization = e.g., profit, output, revenue,

efficiency

###### b. Minimization = e.g., cost, time, waste

*"What resources are limited?" "How does each product use that resource?"*

4. Teaching Non-Negativity Restrictions Instead of stating the rule immediately, ask learners: *"Can the number of products produced be negative?"* *"Can the number of tools rented be negative?"*

###### How to write it:

1. Multiply each decision variable by its contribution to the objective.
2. Add them together to create the objective function.

**a. Write the Constraints**

Constraints represent the limitations in the problem. *These may refer to:*

- Available resources (materials, time, labor, land, budget)
- Maximum production limits
- Storage, safety, or capacity restrictions
- Demand limits

Constraints must be expressed as linear inequalities or

**equations, commonly:**

- "At most" → ≤
- "At least" → ≥
- "No more than" → ≤
- "No less than" → ≥
- "Exactly" → =

###### How to write constraints:

1. Identify the resource being limited.
2. Determine how much of that resource each product uses.
3. Combine them into an inequality.

###### 4. Add the Non-Negativity Restrictions

In real life, we cannot produce negative amounts. Therefore, all variables must be restricted:

**x ≥ 0 y ≥ 0**

5. Consolidating the Model
  a. Once all parts are constructed, show learners how to organize the information clearly.
  b. Structured Representation
  c. Demonstrate how the model is presented using four labeled sections:
    - Decision Variables
    - Objective Function
    - Constraints
    - Non-negativity Conditions/Restrictions

Encourage learners to see the model as a systematic representation of the situation.

6. Deepening Understanding: Conceptual Question Ask the class the reflective question: *"Why is the method called Linear Programming?"* Allow learners to discuss in pairs before sharing answers. Guide the discussion toward the following ideas: *Why Linear?*
    - Because all equations and inequalities involve variables raised only to the first power, forming straight-line relationships. *Why Programming?*
    - Because the method involves planning and organizing decisions systematically to achieve the best result.

Facilitate the second example of writing an LP model through guided practice.

-----

###### Key notes:

- This ensures the solution stays realistic.
- This condition is ALWAYS present in every LP problem.

###### 5. Combine All Parts into a Full LP Model

After identifying variables, objective function, constraints, and non-negativity, we group them logically into one clear model.

###### Example 1. Baking Scenario

A baker wants to determine how many cupcakes and cookies to produce in order to maximize profit. Each cupcake yields a profit of ₱15, while each cookie yields a profit of ₱10. The baker has at most 300 minutes available for production. Producing one cupcake requires 5 minutes, and producing one cookie requires 3 minutes. In addition, the bakery can sell no more than 40 cupcakes.

###### 1. Identify the Decision Variables

###### Example:

A bakery makes cupcakes and cookies. Let:

x = number of cupcakes to produce y = number of cookies to produce

###### 2. Write the Objective Function

###### Example:

Profit = ₱15 per cupcake, ₱10 per cookie

Objective:

###### Maximize P = 15x + 10y

###### 3. Write the Constraints

a. Total baking time available: 300 minutes Cupcake uses 5 minutes; cookie uses 3 minutes Time Constraint: 5x + 3y ≤ 300
b. The bakery can sell no more than 40 cupcakes Demand Constraint: x ≤ 40

-----

###### 4. Add the Non-Negativity Restrictions

x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0

###### 5. Combine All Parts into a Full LP Model

###### LP Model:

Decision Variables: x = cupcakes, y = cookies Objective Function: Maximize P = 15x + 10y, where P is total profit in pesos Constraints:

- **5x + 3y ≤ 300**
- x ≤ 40
- x, y ≥ 0

###### Example 2. Repair Shop

A repair shop needs to minimize its weekly tool rental cost. It rents soldering irons at ₱120 each, with each unit handling 5 repair jobs per week, and multimeters at ₱180 each, with each unit handling 3 repair jobs per week. The shop must complete at least 30 repair jobs weekly, rent at least 4 multimeters for complex work, and can rent no more than 8 soldering irons due to limited supply. Formulate a linear programming model to determine how many of each tool should be rented

###### 1. Decision Variables

Let I = number of soldering irons rented per week *M = number of multimeters rented per week*

**Objective Function (Minimization). Minimize C = 120I +**

180M, where C is the total weekly rental cost in pesos.

**Constraints. Job-capacity requirement: 5I + 3M ≥ 30.**

Minimum multimeters for diagnostics: M ≥ 4. Maximum available soldering irons: I ≤ 8.

**Non-negativity. I ≥ 0, M ≥ 0. LP Model. Decision variables: I = number of soldering irons**

rented per week; M = number of multimeters rented per

-----

week. Objective function: Minimize C = 120I + 180M. Constraints: 5I + 3M ≥ 30; M ≥ 4; I ≤ 8. Non-negativity: I ≥ 0, M ≥ 0.

Question: "Why is it called Linear Programming? Why linear? Why programming?"

###### B.3. Developing Mastery

###### Activity B.3. PRACTICING LEARNED SKILLS

Before facilitating this activity, ensure that all learners have a copy of the LAS

1. Divide the class into groups.
2. Distribute the learning activity sheet.
3. Ask learners to carefully read the instructions and accomplish the tasks accurately.
4. During group discussions, move around the classroom and observe how learners analyze the models.
5. When groups struggle, guide them using probing questions rather than providing direct answers. Examples of guiding questions include:
    a. Does the objective function reflect the goal stated in the problem?
    b. Do the constraints represent the limitations mentioned in the scenario?
    c. Are the inequality symbols consistent with the meaning of the statements?
    d. Do the variables represent the correct quantities in the situation?
6. Encourage learners to revisit the original scenario whenever they are unsure about the correctness of a statement in the model.
7. After the activity, let the learners reflect on their own learning experience by the answering the following questions individually:
  a. What types of errors did you notice most often in the flawed LP models, and how did you identify them?
  b. How did correcting the models help deepen your understanding of how LP problems are properly formulated?
  c. Which part of the LP model (variables, objective function, constraints, or non-negativity) do you still find challenging to analyze or correct? Why?

**III. CONTENT Lesson 4. 2. GRAPHICAL METHOD FOR SOLVING LINEAR PROGRAMMING PROBLEMS**

###### By the end of the lesson, learners are able to:

1. **identify the components of linear programming such as the decision variables, objective function, constraints,** **non-negativity restrictions, and feasible region;** **IV. OBJECTIVES 2. illustrate the feasible region of a Linear Programming (LP) problem;**

###### 3. identify the corner points (vertices) of the feasible region;

4. **determine the optimal solutions to a LP problem using the Corner Point Theorem; and**
5. **solve linear programming problems of two variables by graphical method, with or without technology.** **V. PROCEDURES LEARNERS ACTIVITIES ANNOTATION (FACILITATION GUIDE)** **A. Activating Prior** ***A.1. Eliciting Prior Knowledge*** **Knowledge Activity A.1. LEVELING LEARNER READINESS Activity A.1. LEVELING LEARNER READINESS**

Instruction: Graph the following systems of linear inequalities and identify the shaded region that represents the solution of such inequalities.

Recall the concept of linear inequalities by presenting two examples. Then, facilitate a review of the steps in solving linear inequalities graphically as learners discuss the answers to the guide questions.

-----

1. 3x - y ≤ 2 2x + y < 1 Emphasize that the solution to a system of linear inequalities is

![](img_p204_1.png)

the region where the solution sets of the inequalities overlap. Clarify that if the boundary line is dashed (broken), the points on the line are not included in the solution set; if the boundary line is solid, the points on the line are included in the solution set.

To explain the answers to the activity, let the learners share their thoughts on the importance of graphing systems of linear inequalities. Accept varied responses from the learners.

2. x + 2y ≤ 6 x - y ≥ -1

![](img_p204_2.png)

###### Process Questions:

1. How are linear inequalities in two variables solved graphically?
2. How do you determine whether an ordered pair is a solution to a given linear inequality or a system of linear inequalities?
3. What do you notice about the shaded region when two inequalities overlap, and which part of that overlap represents all possible solutions to the system?

| A.2. Establishing the Purpose of the Lesson |  |
|---|---|
| Activity A. 2. APPRECIATING LESSON RELEVANCE | Activity A. 2. APPRECIATING LESSON RELEVANCE |

###### Instructions:

Read and solve the problem below, then answer the processing questions.

During a school fair, a class plans to sell burgers and hotdogs to raise funds. Each burger yields a profit of ₱10.00, while each hotdog yields a profit of ₱5.00. However, the following constraints must be considered:

1. The booth can prepare at most 80 food items per day.
2. Due to limited ingredients, at most 40 burgers can be prepared.
3. The number of hotdogs must be at most 50 to meet customer demand.

Display the burger / hotdog scenario, then use the process questions to lead learners through naming the decision variables, objective, and constraints in their own words. Do not write the formal LP model on the board yet; the goal is recognition, not formalization. Accept informal phrasing such as 'how many burgers and how many hotdogs' for decision variables.

**Misconception watch. Some learners read 'at most 50 hotdogs'**

as 'exactly 50 hotdogs'. Clarify that 'at most' means '≤ 50, including any value from 0 to 50'.

###### Process Questions:

1. What are the decision variables in the problem?
2. What is the objective function in the problem?
3. What are the limitations or constraints given in the problem?
4. How can these constraints be expressed as mathematical inequalities?
5. If these inequalities are graphed, what points satisfies all the conditions?

The problems below are examples of how to solve linear programming problems. To fully master today's lesson, please listen attentively and actively participate in the discussion.

###### Discussing the Graphical Method Using the Problem

The problem serves as a springboard for discussing the steps in translating constraints into linear inequalities and representing them graphically. Through guided learning, learners identify the feasible region and understand how mathematical models are formed.

Example 1. During a school fair, a class plans to sell burgers and hotdogs to raise funds. Each burger yields a profit of ₱10.00, while each hotdog yields a profit of ₱5.00. However, the following constraints must be considered:

1. The booth can prepare at most 80 food items per day.
2. Due to limited ingredients, at most 40 burgers can be

prepared.

**Example 2.**

A delivery company needs at least 120 liters of fuel per day for its vehicles. The company can purchase gasoline or diesel from a nearby gas station. Gasoline costs ₱62 per liter, while diesel costs ₱57 per liter.

###### Operational Requirements:

1. The company must purchase at least 20 liters of gasoline for motorcycles.
2. The company must purchase at least 30 liters of diesel for delivery vans.
3. The manager aims to determine the number of liters of gasoline and diesel to purchase in order to minimize the total fuel cost

###### Process Questions:

1. What are the decision variables in the problem?
2. What is the objective function of the problem?
3. What are the constraints given in the problem?
4. What are the corner points and the feasible region?

-----

![](img_p213_1.png)

Candidate point A(20, 30) is the intersection of the boundaries x = 20 and y = 30 and is therefore generated by the lowerbound constraints alone. It is not feasible because x + y = 20 + 30 = 50, which is less than the required minimum of 120 liters; the constraint x + y ≥ 120 fails. Do not list A(20, 30) as a corner of the feasible region. The feasible corner points are B(90, 30), from y = 30 and x + y = 120, and C(20, 100), from x = 20 and x + y = 120.

5. What is the optimal solution? = 62 + 57

| Corner | (x,y) | Total cost |
|---|---|---|
| A | (20,30) | Not feasible |
| B | (90,30) | 62(90) + 57(30) = 5580 +1710 = 7290 |
| C | (20,100) | 62(20) + 57(100) = 1240 + 5700 = 6940 |

###### Optimal Solution

The minimum cost is ₱6,940, which occurs at (20, 100). This means that the company should purchase 20 liters of gasoline and 100 liters of diesel to achieve the minimum total fuel cost of ₱6,940.

###### B.1. Presenting Examples

###### B. Instituting New

###### Activity B.1. EXPLORING KEY CONCEPTS Knowledge Instructions:

To show you how to solve a real-life linear programming problem, let's use a classic business scenario: maximizing

**profit under resource constraints.**

**Situation: A small bakery produces loaves of bread and**

cakes. Each loaf earns a profit of Php30, while each cake earns Php40. The bakery wants to maximize its total profit. However, production is limited by the following resources:

1. Each loaf requires 2 hours of labor and each cake requires 1 hour. The company has at most 10 labor hours available per day.
2. Each loaf and cake both use 1 unit of raw material, and only 7 units are available.
3. Each loaf uses 1 unit of another material, while each cake uses 2 units. There only 12 units available.

###### Activity B.1. EXPLORING KEY CONCEPTS

Guide the learners to translate the real-life situation into linear programming model.

The problem is a linear programming maximization problem with three inequality constraints and two variables. To solve it using the Simplex method, we first convert inequalities into equalities by adding slack variables. Then, we set up the initial simplex tableau and perform pivot operations to optimize the objective function.

In Step 1, after writing the objective function and constraints, decision variables are represented as , , 3 … to prepare

1 2

students for the simplex method. Although earlier sections may use and , explain that these are simply renamed as 1and 2 . The change is intentional and supports the simplex method's ability to handle multiple variables systematically.

**Step 1. Set up the problem. Write the objective function and**

the constraints. Since the simplex method is used for problems that consists many variables, we can use symbols

| , | , | , and so on. |
|---|---|---|
| 1 Let | 2 | 3 |

1 = loaves of bread 2= pieces of cakes

The problem is formulated the same way as we did for graphical methods. Maximize: = 30 1 + 40 2 Subject to:

###### (Standard form) Inequalities are converted into equalities by

adding non-negative slack or surplus variables.

###### (Tableau Method) Calculations are typically organized in a

"simplex tableau" to perform row operations and check for optimality.

The objective-row entries are written as the negatives of the original objective coefficients because we rearrange the objective Z = c1 x1 + c2 x2 + ... as Z - c1 x1 - c2 x2 - ... = 0 before putting it in the tableau. This is bookkeeping, not a maxto-min conversion: the simplex method is solving the original maximization problem directly.
