**Step 8. Repeat - Identify the entering variable.**

-----

Look for the most negative coefficient in the Z-row: the only negative entry is -10 (under x1). So x1 enters the basis.

**Step 9. Identify the leaving variable.**

Compute the minimum ratio of RHS to the positive entries in the x1 column: For s1 row: 4 ÷ (3/2) = 8/3 ≈ 2.67 For s2 row: 1 ÷ (1/2) = 2 For x2 row: 6 ÷ (1/2) = 12 Minimum ratio is 2 (s2 row), so s2 leaves the basis.

**Step 10. Pivot on the (s2 row, x1 column) entry.**

Divide the s2 row by the pivot value 1/2 to make the pivot equal to 1; the s2 row becomes the new x1 row: [1, 0, 0, 2, -1, 0, 2].

**Step 11. Make the other entries in the x1 column zero, then read off the solution.**

After elimination, the final tableau is:

s1: [ 0, 0, 1, -3, 1, 0, 1] x1: [ 1, 0, 0, 2, -1, 0, 2] x2: [ 0, 1, 0, -1, 1, 0, 5] Z: [ 0, 0, 0, 20, 10, 1, 260] All Z-row coefficients are non-negative, so the solution is optimal. Basic variables: x1 = 2, x2 = 5, s1 = 1. Non-basic: s2 = s3 = 0. Maximum value of Z (profit P) = 260.

**Conclusion.**

The bakery should produce 2 loaves of bread and 5 pieces of cakes to make a maximum profit of ₱260.

###### B.2. Discussing the Concept

**Activity B.2. DEEPENING CONCEPTUAL UNDERSTANDING Activity B.2. DEEPENING CONCEPTUAL UNDERSTANDING**

###### Instructions:

1. Begin the discussion by reviewing the conditions required The problems are examples of solving LP using simplex method. for applying the Simplex Method. Ask learners to examine To understand the process listen attentively and participate the given linear programming problem and verify that it actively during discussion. satisfies all the conditions for the method. Emphasize the role of the objective function, constraints, non-negativity

###### Scope and Conditions of the Simplex Method

restrictions, and slack variables.

-----

1. The LP must be a MAXIMIZATION problem (we maximize Z = c1 x1 + c2 x2 + ... + ck xk).
2. Every structural constraint must be of the form a x 1 + b x2 + ... ≤ r, with the right-hand side r non-negative.
3. Every decision variable must satisfy non-negativity: x1, x2, ..., xk ≥ 0.
4. There is one slack variable per ≤ constraint, so the slack variables form the initial basis with values equal to the RHS of each constraint.

**Solve using the Simplex method.**

Maximize: Z = 10x1 + 15x2 + 20x3 Subject to constraints: 2x1 + 4x2 + 6x3 ≤ 24 3x1 + 9x2 + 6x3 ≤ 30 x1, x2, x3 ≥ 0

**Step 1. Set up the problem.**

Maximize: Z = 10x1 + 15x2 + 20x3 Subject to: 2x1 + 4x2 + 6x3 ≤ 24 3x1 + 9x2 + 6x3 ≤ 30 x1, x2, x3 ≥ 0

**Step 2. Introduce slack variables. Add slack variables s1 and**

s2 to convert the ≤ constraints into equations.

###### Standard form:

2x1 + 4x2 + 6x3 + s1 = 24 3x1 + 9x2 + 6x3 + s2 = 30 Z - 10x1 - 15x2 - 20x3 = 0

x1, x2, x3, s1, s2 ≥ 0

3. Guide learners in converting the inequalities into equations by introducing slack variables. Facilitate a discussion on why slack variables are necessary and how they help establish the initial basic feasible solution.
4. Display the initial simplex tableau and model how to i identify the entering and leaving variables. Ask probing questions that help learners justify their choices using the most negative entry in the objective row and the minimumratio test.
5. Lead learners through each pivoting operation. As learners perform row operations, encourage them to explain how the pivot row is normalized and how the remaining entries in the pivot column are eliminated. Monitor their work and address misconceptions related to tableau manipulation.
6. Facilitate a whole-class discussion of the final tableau. Guide learners in interpreting the values of the basic and non-basic variables, determining the maximum value of the objective function, and verifying that the solution satisfies all original constraints. Conclude by discussing how the Simplex Method provides an efficient way to solve optimization problems involving multiple variables and constraints.

###### Process Questions:

1. Which row shows the objective function?
2. What does the final value of Z represent?
3. Why is the solution considered optimal?

**Step 3. Set up the initial simplex tableau.**

| Basis | x1 | x2 | x3 | s1 | s2 | RHS |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| s1 | 2 | 4 | 6 | 1 | 0 | 24 |
| s2 | 3 | 9 | 6 | 0 | 1 | 30 |
| Z | -10 | -15 | -20 | 0 | 0 | 0 |

-----

**Step 4. Identify the entering variable. The most negative Z-**

row entry is -20 under x3, so x3 enters the basis.

**Step 5. Identify the leaving variable. Apply the minimum-**

ratio test to the x3 column: 24/6 = 4 and 30/6 = 5. The smallest positive ratio is 4, so s1 leaves the basis. The pivot element is 6.

**Step 6. Normalize the pivot row by dividing the s1 row by**

6. **The new x3 row is [1/3, 2/3, 1, 1/6, 0 | 4].**

**Step 7. Eliminate the other entries in the x3 column.**

R2\_new = R2 - 6·R1\_new. Z\_new = Z + 20·R1\_new.

###### Tableau after iteration 1

| Basis | x1 | x2 | x3 | s1 | s2 | RHS |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| x1 | 1/2 | 2/3 | 1 | 1/6 | 0 | 4 |
| s2 | 1 | 5 | 0 | -1 | 1 | 6 |
| Z | -10/3 | -5/3 | 0 | 10/3 | 0 | 80 |

**Step 8. Repeat the entering-variable test. The most negative**

Z-row entry is -10/3 under x1, so x1 enters the basis.

**Step 9. Identify the leaving variable. Apply the minimum-**

ratio test to the x1 column: for the x3 row, 4 / (1/3) = 12; for the s2 row, 6 / 1 = 6. The smallest positive ratio is 6, so s2 leaves the basis. The pivot element is 1.

**Step 10. Pivot and eliminate the other x1-column entries.**

The s2 row already has pivot value 1, so it becomes the new x 1 row. R1\_new = R1 - (1/3)·R2\_new. Z\_new = Z + (10/3)·R2\_new.

###### Final tableau:

| Basis | x1 | x2 | x3 | s1 | s2 | RHS |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| x3 | 0 | -1 | 1 | 1/2 | -1/3 | 2 |
| x1 | 1 | 5 | 0 | -1 | 1 | 6 |
| Z | 0 | 15 | 0 | 0 | 10 | 100 |

**Step 11. Check optimality and read the solution. All Z-row**

entries are non-negative, so the solution is optimal. Basic variables: x1 = 6 and x3 = 2. Non-basic variable: x2 = 0. Maximum value: Z = 100.

| Knowledge and Skills | Activity C.1. MAKING REAL-WORLD CONNECTIONS Instructions: 1. Read and analyze the problem. Identify the variables, objective function, and constraints based on the available labor hours and fertilizer units. 2. Write the mathematical model, including the profit function and all constraints. 3. Graph the constraints on a coordinate plane and determine the feasible region. 4. Identify the corner points of the feasible region and evaluate the profit at each point. 5. Determine the combination of tomatoes and eggplants that maximizes profit. Explain and justify the solution using the graph and computations. SMART FARMING FOR MAXIMUM PROFIT Mang Roberto supplies vegetables to a local market and a small grocery. He grows tomatoes and eggplants and wants to maximize his profit. Each crop requires different resources and provides different profits. Tomatoes earn a profit of ₱500 per crate, while eggplants earn ₱400 per crate. | Activity C.1. MAKING REAL-WORLD CONNECTIONS 1. Present the situation and ask learners to identify the decision that Mang Roberto needs to make. Guide them in defining the variables, objective function, and constraints. 2. Facilitate a discussion on how the information in the problem can be translated into mathematical expressions. 3. Check learners' formulations and provide support when needed. 4. Instruct learners to graph the constraints and identify the feasible region. Move around the room to monitor progress and clarify misconceptions about graphing and interpreting constraints. 6. Guide learners in determining the corner points of the feasible region and evaluating the profit function at each point. Encourage them to explain their computations and reasoning. 8. Lead a synthesis discussion by asking learners to justify the optimal solution and explain how linear programming can help make informed decisions in real-life situations such as farm management and resource allocation Processing Questions 1. How did the labor and fertilizer constraints affect the number of crates of tomatoes and eggplants that could be |

###### C.2. Making Generalization

###### Activity C.2. WRAPPING UP THE LESSON

###### Instructions:

Complete the organizer below to summarize the key concepts learned in solving linear programming problems using the simplex method. Questions How can we determine the best possible solution when there are multiple constraints? Why is it important to optimize resources in real-life situations? How does the simplex method improve decision-making compared to graphing methods? What does an "optimal solution" mean and how do we reach it?

***C.3. Evaluating Learning (For the complete instructions for the learners, please refer to the LAS.)***

###### Activity C.2. WRAPPING UP THE LESSON

1. Distribute or display the organizer and ask learners to r review the activities completed during the lesson on linear programming and the simplex method.
2. Guide learners in reflecting on their learning by discussing Responses each question briefly before they write their responses. Encourage them to connect the concepts to the activities they have completed.
4. Provide sufficient time for individual completion of the organizer. Move around the room to monitor progress and clarify misconceptions about optimization, constraints, and the simplex method.
5. Facilitate a sharing session by inviting selected learners or groups to present their responses. Encourage learners to compare ideas and provide mathematical justifications for their answers.
6. Synthesize the lesson by highlighting how the simplex method identifies optimal solutions efficiently and supports decision-making in situations involving limited resources and multiple constraints.

###### Activity C.3. ASSESSING LEARNING OUTCOMES

Before facilitating this activity, ensure that all learners have a copy of the LAS

1. To assess learners' understanding of the lesson, facilitate the evaluation activity. After reviewing their responses, guide learners in a brief reflection to evaluate their learning and identify areas for further improvement.
2. Invite selected learners to share their responses to encourage reflection and discussion. Learners' answers are used as

Based on the results of the evaluation, facilitate the appropriate learning activities. Learners who have not yet attained mastery should complete the remediation activity, while those who have attained mastery should be provided with the enhancement activity.

During the remediation activity, teacher support is essential. Provide timely guidance, feedback, and scaffolding to help learners address misconceptions, strengthen their understanding of the concepts, and progress toward mastery.
